S5
Additional Data Application Information

S5.1
Methodological Details

In this section, we provide some additional details on the data application which were omitted from the main text due to space constraints.

Model Specifications

•

\bm{L}^{*}
: surgery site, race, sex, age, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), self reported smoking status, hypertension, dyslipidemia, and calendar year of surgery

•

\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
: baseline BMI, baseline A1C, T2DM medication usage, DiaRem score (remission outcome only)

•

A
: RYGB (
A=1
) vs. SG (
A=0
)

•

Y
:
\%
weight change at 3 years post surgery (continuous), remission of T2DM at any point within 3 years post surgery (binary)

As mentioned in the main text, T2DM status is a discrete covariate which has no variance among the study eligible population, and thus it is not included in modeling any component nuisance functions, even though it may reasonably be considered in
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
if desired. While several medication types were considered for establishment of T2DM status, when modeling nuisance functions, we only considered an indicator of insulin usage in the corresponding lookback window, as insulin is typically utilized when other common T2DM medications fail to achieve desired level of glycemic control
(
Thota and Akbar, 2023
)
.

\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{IF}}
were estimated with the SL1 set of SuperLearner libraries. For nuisance functions with continuous modeling targets
(\mu,\xi,\gamma,\chi,\nu)
, these libraries included random forest (
SL.ranger
), linear models (
SL.lm
), and multivariate adaptive polynomial regression spline (
SL.polymars
). For binary modeling targets
(\eta,u,\varepsilon,\omega)
, these libraries included random forest (
SL.ranger
), generalized linear models (
SL.glm
), and generalized additive models (
SL.gam
). Rather than just use a single candidate random forest learner, all nuisance models used 27 candidate random forest learners over a grid of three important hyperparameters.

•

mtry
(number of predictors that will be randomly sampled at each split):
\{0.5,1,2\}\times\lfloor\sqrt{p}\rfloor
, where
p
denotes the rank of the corresponding design matrix

•

num.trees
(number of trees used for prediction):
\{250,500,1000\}

•

min.node.size
(minimal node size to split at):
\{5,30,50\}

Finally, both outcome model
\mu
and propensity score
u
were trained only using eligible subjects. Given that the contributions of these nuisance functions to
\beta(P)
are only non-zero for eligible subjects, this is strictly a decision about whether one believes they can model these contributions better by including ineligible subjects, and thus increasing sample size, or by getting a smaller but more narrow training set for the model which better reflects the patient who ultimately contribute.

\widehat{\theta}_{\text{CC}}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{IWOR}}
used a linear model for the outcome regression, also fit among ascertainably eligible subjects by definition. The following
A\times\bm{L}
interactions were specified, motivated by existing bariatric surgery literature
(
Arterburn et al., 2020
;
McTigue et al., 2020
)
.

•

% Weight Change: Sex, Age, Race, Baseline BMI

•

Diabetes Remission: Sex, Age, Baseline A1C, DiaRem

Additional Missing Data Considerations

The focus of this work was on missingness in eligibility defining covariates
\bm{L}^{e}
, and thus implicit to this work was the assumption that there was no missingness in other variables. In practice, in EHR-based observational studies, there is likely to be missingness elsewhere, which was the case in our data application. While the missingness was not nearly as pervasive as in eligibility defining covariates, we describe additional missing data challenges and our approach in dealing with such challenges in the data application.

In our application on bariatric surgery, there was no missingness in treatment
A
, which may not be the case in all applications. Only a single covariate in
\bm{L}^{*}
, baseline eGFR, was missing for a small number of subjects. Estimated glomerular filtration rate is a function of serum creatinine, age, and sex
(
National Kidney Foundation, 2021
)
, so whenever eGFR was missing it was because a patient’s serum creatinine value was not available. Similar to previous work by
Benz et al. (2024)
, we imputed missing serum creatinine values (prior to operationalizing the eligibility criteria) using a gamma GLM and used the imputed value to compute eGFR.

Recall that covariates
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
are used in two nuisance functions,
\mu_{a}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
and
u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
. In our application, we estimated these models using eligible complete cases
(E=1,R=1)
rather than all complete cases
(R=1)
given that their respective influence function contributions are multiplied by
E
and thus do not contribute if
E=0
. Nevertheless, because T2DM status could be established without a baseline A1C measure for a small subset of patients, baseline A1C was not available for use as an important confounder. For study eligible patients without available A1C, we imputed A1C values using a gamma GLM as in previous work
(
Benz et al., 2024
)
. Imputation was not used to retroactively determine study eligibility (eg., to determine T2DM status or DiaRem score).

Finally, weight change at 3 years was computed following the method of
Thaweethai et al. (2021)
, and could be missing if patients were missing a baseline BMI and/or were missing a weight measure within
\pm
6 months of 3 years post-surgery. Given that outcomes
Y
are in the conditioning set for several nuisance functions used by
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
, we decided to impute weight outcomes when missing so that we could illustrate the use of
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
. Table
S3
reports the number of imputed values for each variable across the 40 operationalizations of the eligibility criteria. Baseline eGFR was imputed for 108 patients (0.7% of the full analytic cohort). Across operationalizations of eligibility status, baseline A1c was imputed, among eligible complete cases (
E=1
,
R=1
), for 33 to 2,189 patients depending on the A1c lookback window; expressed as a percentage of the full analytic cohort for each operationalization, this corresponds to 0.2% to 14.8%. Three-year percent weight change was imputed for 3,519 - 5,446 patients (23.8%-36.8% of the full cohort), and 619 - 948 patients (20.4%-22.6% of eligible complete cases; e.g., where the outcome has greater impact in the influence function for
\beta(P)
).

In all cases, the fraction of imputed values was far smaller than the fraction of patients with missing eligibility information (Section 2.2). In other applications, outcome missingness might be more pervasive, which might motivate analysts to consider using
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{IF}}
instead of
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
, as the former does not use
Y
in the conditioning set of any component nuisance function. We used a two tiered approach towards imputing outcomes: if baseline BMI was available, we imputed
\%
weight change at 3 years via linear regression using
A,\bm{L}^{*}
, and baseline BMI as predictors; if baseline BMI was not available we imputed
\%
weight change at 3 years via linear regression using
A,\bm{L}^{*}
.

Table S3:
Imputation counts by lookback scenario, n (%).

That our (eligibility) complete case analysis closely matches the results reported by
McTigue et al. (2020)
suggests that the small amount of imputation did not substantively drive any observed results. Moreover,
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{IF}}
does not condition on
Y
in any nuisance function, and so does not depend on imputed outcomes through the nested functions
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\xi
,
\gamma
and
\chi
. The clinical conclusions from
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{IF}}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
are consistent (Figure 5), which further suggests that outcome imputation did not drive the results. We did not propagate imputation uncertainty into the reported confidence intervals, as missing outcomes and non-eligibility covariates are not the focus of this work and a bootstrap over the entire estimation pipeline is computationally prohibitive.

Table S5:
Comparison of estimators of
\theta(P)
on 3-year diabetes remission.

Tables
S4
and
S5
present full point estimates 95% confidence intervals for 3-year weight change and T2DM remission differences, respectively. For the weight change outcome, we also present E-values
(
Haneuse et al., 2019
;
VanderWeele and Ding, 2017
)
, which has been used in DURABLE studies
(
Harrington et al., 2024
;
Fisher et al., 2018
;
Coleman et al., 2022
;
O’Brien et al., 2018
)
as way to quantify the strength of unmeasured confounding that would be required to overturn observed results. Because the E-value was derived in the context of risk ratio, and we estimate an ATT-type contrast for a continuous outcome, we apply the approximation outlined in
(
VanderWeele and Ding, 2017
)
,
RR\approx\exp(0.91\times\text{SMD})
where SMD denotes the standardized mean difference. We take SMD
=\widehat{\theta}/\widehat{\sigma}_{Y}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{Y}
outcome standard deviation among eligible complete cases. Given that all E-values exceed 2, an unmeasured confounder would need to be associated with both the exposure (bariatric surgery) and 3-year weight change by a risk ratio of at least 2-fold each, above and beyond the measured covariates
\bm{L}^{*}
and
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
, to fully explain away the observed treatment effect. This magnitude of confounding exceeds that observed for any of our measured covariates and is not thought to characterize unmeasured factors in other DURABLE studies
(
Harrington et al., 2024
;
Fisher et al., 2018
;
Coleman et al., 2022
;
O’Brien et al., 2018
)
, lending support to the robustness of our estimates to plausible unmeasured confounding.

S6
Sensitivity Analysis

S6.1
Sensitivity Analysis for Assumption 4 (MAR)

In order to explore the impact of certain violations of Assumption 4, we conduct a comprehensive sensitivity analysis. In particular, we follow the general ideas of
Scharfstein et al. (1999)
.

First, observe that Assumption 4, which states that
R\perp\!\!\!\perp(\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y)~|~\bm{L}^{*},A
, implies that

P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,Y,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})=P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A)

To formally explore violations of Assumption 4 to certain forms of MNAR, we suppose that

\text{logit}\bigr[P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,Y,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\bigr]=b(\bm{L}^{*},A)+h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y;\bm{\alpha})

(S2)

for some functions
b(\cdot)

h(\cdot)
and sensitivity parameter(s)
\bm{\alpha}
, where
h(\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,;\bm{0})\equiv 0
. Note that Assumption 4 holds when
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{0}
. (In this section only,
\bm{\alpha}
denotes the vector of sensitivity parameters, and should not be confused with the functional
\alpha(P)
that appears in the denominator of
\theta(P)=\beta(P)/\alpha(P)
.)

Given that both clinical outcomes examined reflect changes from baseline (% weight change from study baseline or remission of baseline diabetes status), we work just with
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
. In particular, we take
h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})
to be

h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})=\alpha_{\text{A1C}}(\text{Baseline A1C}-6.5\%)+\alpha_{\text{BMI}}(\text{Baseline BMI}-35\text{kg/m}^{2})

where
\alpha_{\text{A1C}}
and
\alpha_{\text{BMI}}
can interpreted as the log-odds of having completely observed eligibility information, per unit change in A1C/BMI, relative to their respective eligibility-defining thresholds.

Because
h
depends only on
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
and not on
Y
, this sensitivity model perturbs the ascertainment probability only through the eligibility-defining covariates
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
, holding
\bm{L}^{*}
and
A
fixed. As such, it addresses departures from Assumption 4 in which ascertainment depends on
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
given
\bm{L}^{*}
and
A
; it does not address missingness that depends on the outcome
Y
itself, given
\bm{L}^{*}
,
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
, and
A
.

Under the assumed sensitivity model in Equation (
S2
), and the fact that
h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})
does not depend on
Y
, we have

\text{logit}\bigr[P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\bigr]=b(\bm{L}^{*},A)+h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})

Now, notice that by the law of iterated expectation,

\mathbb{E}\Bigr[P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~\bm{L}^{*},A\Bigr]=P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A)=\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\frac{R}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},A\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\frac{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},A\Bigr]\implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\frac{R}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},A\Bigr]

\displaystyle=1\implies

\displaystyle\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\frac{1}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\Bigr]

\displaystyle=1~~~(\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma:bvt}})

Solving for
b(\bm{L}^{*},A)
for a given sensitivity parameter
\bm{\alpha}
, we have that

\displaystyle P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\exp[-b(\bm{L}^{*},A)-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})]}\implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}

\displaystyle=1+\exp[-b(\bm{L}^{*},A)-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})]\implies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)\times\mathbb{E}\Bigr[1+\exp[-b(\bm{L}^{*},A)-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})]~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\Bigr]\implies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)\Biggr(1+\exp[-b(\bm{L}^{*},A)]\times\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\exp\bigr(-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})\bigr)~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\Bigr]\Biggr)\implies

\displaystyle\exp[-b(\bm{L}^{*},A)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)\times\mathbb{E}\Bigr[\exp\bigr(-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})\bigr)~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\Bigr]}

Thus, under the assumed sensitivity model, that

P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})=\Biggr[1+\frac{1-\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)}\times\frac{\exp[-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})]}{\mathbb{E}\bigr[\exp\bigr(-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})\bigr)~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\bigr]}\Biggr]^{-1}

(S3)

The idea for the sensitivity analysis is to replace
P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A)
by
P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
in the expression to estimate
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
. First, note that to estimate
P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
under this sensitivity model, we need to estimate one additional nuisance function,
\mathbb{E}\bigr[\exp\bigr(-h(\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})\bigr)~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,R=1\bigr]
, which can be estimated from the observed data. Once
P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
is computed for every complete case, it can replace
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)
in
nearly
every location in Equations (3) and (4). This is due to the fact the majority of terms where
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)
appears, it is pre-multiplied by the complete case indicator
R
, and thus only contributes to the influence function contribution if the subject has
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
observed.

However, the term
\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}
would at first appear to be problematic to replace by
\frac{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}
,given that this term is contributed by all subjects, not just complete cases, and the ratio depends on
\bm{L}_{m}^{e}
. Instead, we replace the two augmentation terms in
Equation (4) involving
\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
, namely

(1-A)\left[\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}-\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\right]\Big(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Big),

by the single term

(1-A)\,\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\left(\frac{R}{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}-1\right)\Big(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Big)

(S4)

When
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{0}
, Equation(
S4
) reduces exactly to the original two terms. Moreover, Equation (
S4
) has mean zero under the sensitivity model, since
R\perp\!\!\!\perp Y\mid\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}_{m}^{e}
implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{R}{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}\;\middle|\;\bm{L}^{*},A=0,Y\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}{\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}\;\middle|\;\bm{L}^{*},A=0,Y\right]=1.

This swap may affect efficiency under violations of MAR, but not unbiasedness when the
sensitivity model is correct. Critically, the only component of Equation (
S4
) that depends on
\bm{L}_{m}^{e}
is premultiplied by
R
; for subjects with
R=0
, Equation (
S4
) depends only on
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)
and
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)
. Formal justification that
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
remains unbiased under the procedure below is provided in Section
S6.2
.

Instead, we replace the augmentation term in Equation (4),
(1-A)\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\Bigr(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)
by

(1-A)\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\Biggr(\frac{R}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}-1{}\Biggr)\Bigr(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)

This swap keeps this term mean 0, which may impact the efficiency under violations of MAR, but not the unbiasedness (if the sensitivity model is correct). The unbiasedness of
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
under the sensitivity model is demonstrated in Section
S6.2
. Critically, this term as the component dependent on
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
is premultipled by
R
.

To query the sensitivity of
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
, we take the following steps:

1.

Estimate
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
following Algorithm 1 retaining the cross-fitted nuisance estimates
\widehat{\eta}
,
\widehat{u}
,
\widehat{\mu}_{0}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}
,
\widehat{\xi}
,
\widehat{\gamma}
, and
\widehat{\chi}
for each subject.

2.

For a given pair
(\alpha_{\text{A1C}},\alpha_{\text{BMI}})
, compute
h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})
for all patients with completely observed eligibility information (
R=1
).

3.

Within each training fold of Algorithm 1, estimate the regression
\kappa(\bm{L}^{*},a;\bm{\alpha})=\mathbb{E}\big[\exp\big(-h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})\big)\mid\bm{L}^{*},A=a,R=1\big]
, and obtain predictions
\widehat{\kappa}(\bm{L}^{*},0;\bm{\alpha})
and
\widehat{\kappa}(\bm{L}^{*},1;\bm{\alpha})
for all subjects in the corresponding held-out fold.

4.

For patients with completely observed eligibility information (
R=1
), compute

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\left[1+\frac{1-\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)}{\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\times\frac{\exp\big[-h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})\big]}{\widehat{\kappa}(\bm{L}^{*},1;\bm{\alpha})}\right]^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\left[1+\frac{1-\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)}\times\frac{\exp\big[-h(\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})\big]}{\widehat{\kappa}(\bm{L}^{*},0;\bm{\alpha})}\right]^{-1}.

5.

In Equations (3) and (4), replace
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)
by
\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})
in every term premultiplied by
R
, including the terms premultiplied by
(1-A)R
, and replace the two
\big(\widehat{\gamma}Y-\widehat{\chi}\big)
augmentation terms by Equation (
S4
). All other nuisance estimates
(
\widehat{u}
,
\widehat{\mu}_{0}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}
,
\widehat{\xi}
,
\widehat{\gamma}
,
\widehat{\chi}
) are unchanged. Recompute
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
following steps 5 and 6 of Algorithm 1.

6.

Repeat steps 2–5 over a grid of
(\alpha_{\text{A1C}},\alpha_{\text{BMI}})
.

At
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{0}
,
\widehat{\kappa}\equiv 1
and
\widetilde{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{0})=\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},A)
, so the procedure reproduces
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
from Algorithm 1 exactly. Finally, We note that other types of MNAR violations of Assumption 4 are possible, but such violations are not easy to explore, particularly with interpretable sensitivity parameters, which play an important role in describing the robustness of clinical conclusions to MNAR violations of this form. Formal justification that
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
remains unbiased when under the above procedure is provided in Section
S6.2
.

Results over a grid of
(\alpha_{\text{A1C}},\alpha_{\text{BMI}})
are presented in Figures
S5
-
S8
. In particular, each cell plots
point estimates
for
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
under each respective pair of sensitivity parameters. Cells in red favor RYGB, cells in blue favor SG, and cells in white reflect that point estimates very close to 0. Thus, the white regions separating red and blue cells can be viewed as the a “tipping point” region for the point estimate of
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}

For the weight change outcome, in the most stringent of BMI/A1C lookback windows, no combination of
(\alpha_{\text{A1C}},\alpha_{\text{BMI}})
changed the point estimate in favor of SG. More extreme values of of both sensitivity parameters were considered but such exponential tilt led to extreme positivity violations which made
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
impossible to compute. The confidence interval crossed 0 for values of
\alpha_{\text{BMI}}<-0.14
. Such an extreme sensitivity value implies patients with baseline BMI of
60
kg/m
2
(a threshold exceeded by 313 patients just 1 month prior to surgery) would have an odds ratio of ¡ 0.05. That is, in order to obtain a confidence interval consistent with no weight loss difference between the two surgical procedures, patients with the most severe obesity would need to have at least 1/20
{}^{\text{th}}
the odds of ascertainable eligibility compared to otherwise identical patients with BMI of
35
kg/m
2
.

By contrast, for the T2DM remission outcome, the difference in T2DM remission rate can be made to favor SG with much smaller values of
\alpha_{\text{BMI}}
across the entire spectrum of
\alpha_{\text{A1C}}
, at least for shorter A1C lookback windows. For example with
\alpha_{\text{A1C}}=0
, the point estimate can be made to favor SG under
\alpha_{\text{BMI}}=-0.03
, which corresponds to an odds ratio of 0.97 per 1 kg/m
2
of having ascertainable eligibility status. This is not surprising given that
\theta_{\text{EIF}}
for the T2DM outcome is closer to the null and the confidence interval already contains 0. Such results substantiate our interpretation that remission of T2DM should be an auxiliary consideration when deciding between SG and RYGB, at least relative to desired weight loss, along with surgical safety.

Sensitivity analysis for Assumption 4 (MAR): 3-Year Weight Change (T2DM ascertainment via prescriptions requires active prescription at the time of surgery)

S6.2
Proof of Unbiasedness Under Sensitivity Model

In this section, we offer justification that the procedure outlined in Section
S6.1
preserves unbiased estimation of
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
under correctly specified sensitivity model (which we assume and query under a large number of sensitivity models).

In the remainder of this section we let

\eta(\,\cdot,\bm{\alpha})\equiv\mathbb{P}[R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y]

In Section
S6.1
, we conducted sensitivity analysis only with respect
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
, given that both clinical outcomes examined reflect changes from baseline (% weight change from study baseline or remission of baseline diabetes status). Implicitly, this invokes the assumption that
R\perp\!\!\!\perp Y~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A
.
Thus,
\eta(\,\cdot,\bm{\alpha})
does not depend on
Y
and
\mathbb{P}[R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y]=\mathbb{P}[R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m}]
. We then show that replacing
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A)
by our sensitivity model
\eta(\,\cdot,\bm{\alpha})
in uncentered influence functions
\dot{\alpha}_{P}(O)
(not to be confused with sensitivity parameter
\bm{\alpha}
) and
\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)
preserve

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{\alpha}_{P}(O)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[EA]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[EA]\times\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}

Expectation of
\dot{\alpha}_{P}(O)
Under Ascertainment Probability Substitution

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{\alpha}_{P}(O)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggr[A\Bigr(1-\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\Bigr)\varepsilon_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)+\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggr[A\Bigr(1-\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\Bigr)\varepsilon_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)+\frac{AE\times\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\biggr]~~~\text{(It. Exp. $\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A,Y$)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[EA]

Expectation of
\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)
Under Ascertainment Probability Substitution

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Biggr\{\frac{AR}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\biggr[\Bigr(E-\varepsilon_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)Y-\Bigr(E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})-\xi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle+A\Bigr(\varepsilon_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\xi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-A)R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\biggr[E\frac{u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{1-u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}\Bigr(Y-\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\Bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle+(1-A)\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\Biggr(\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}-1{}\Biggr)\Bigr(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)\Biggr\}

After applying iterated expectation conditioning on
\bm{L}^{*},A,\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y
, the first 2 terms simplify to

\mathbb{E}\Bigr[EA[Y-\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})]\Bigr]

and

\mathbb{E}\biggr\{\frac{(1-A)R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}\biggr[E\frac{u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{1-u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}\Bigr(Y-\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\Bigr)\biggr]\biggr\}=0

Thus, we are left with

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Biggr\{EA[Y-\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})]

\displaystyle-(1-A)\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\Biggr(\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}^{e}_{m};\bm{\alpha})}-1{}\Biggr)\Bigr(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Bigr)\Biggr\}

Finally, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggr[(1-A)\,\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\left(\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}-1\right)\Big(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Big)\biggr]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggr[(1-A)\,\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\left(\frac{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A=0,\bm{L}^{e}_{m},Y)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}-1\right)\Big(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Big)\biggr]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggr[(1-A)\,\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\left(\frac{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0,\bm{L}_{m}^{e};\bm{\alpha})}-1\right)\Big(\gamma(\bm{L}^{*},Y)Y-\chi(\bm{L}^{*},Y)\Big)\biggr]=0

The last line follows as
Y\perp\!\!\!\perp R~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A
under the working sensitivity model.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y(0)~|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~\text{(A2, A3)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~\text{(A1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~A=0,E,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~\text{($E=g(\bm{L}^{e})$ fixed)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~Y\perp\!\!\!\perp R~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A~\text{(sensitivity model)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~A=1]}{P(E=1~|~A=1)}~~~\text{(Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt})}

Crucially, that we can apply
Y\perp\!\!\!\perp R~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A
is because of the assumed sensitivity model rather than Assumption 4. Thus,
\mathbb{E}[\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)]=\mathbb{E}[EA]\times\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
, and so the sensitivity procedure in Section
S6.1
is unbiased under the assumed sensitivity model.

S7
Practitioner Guidance

The proposed analysis framework centered
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
has many moving parts, by design. Given such moving parts, it might be difficult for practitioners to evaluate estimator stability and diagnose potential causes for concern. In this section, we offer some general guidance and considerations for implementing
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
. In particular, we list several diagnostic checks an analyst might consider when implementing
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
. While we cannot offer hard and fast rules, we offer some general commentary on possible causes for concern. We conclude by conducting the checks in our analysis of 3-year weight change following surgery.

S7.1
Diagnostic Checklist

•

Check
: Distribution of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: Assumption 5 (Complete Case Positivity)

–

Reason
: Division by
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)
in component influence functions
\dot{\alpha}_{P}(O)
and
\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)

–

What to look for
:
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)
can be interpreted as an inverse probability of ascertainment weight, so analysts should examine
\min\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)
. Causes for concern include extremely small weights (e.g.,
\min\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)<0.01
), or a large concentration of small weights (e.g. at least 25% of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)\leq 0.1)
.

•

Check
: Distribution of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1),\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)
together

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: —

–

Reason
: Differential missingness by treatment status

–

What to look for
: If there are large areas where the distributions of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)
and
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)
do not overlap, this suggests that missingness in eligibility-defining criteria is differential by treatment status, even after accounting for completely observed covariates. While differential missingness does not necessarily imply violations of Assumption 4 (MAR), it would be worth noting and may give analysts reason to select alternative lookback windows where missingness is not differential by treatment status (after accounting for
\bm{L}^{*}
). A reasonable diagnostic to check and report is
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)-\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)]
.

•

Check
: Effective Sample Size (ESS)

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: Assumption 5 (Complete Case Positivity)

–

Reason
: Calibration of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)

–

What to look for
: Following the ideas of
Kish (1992)
and
Ho et al. (2011)
, an approximate ESS based on the inverse probability of ascertainment weights is given as follows,

ESS=\frac{\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}E_{i}\times 1/\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*}_{i},1)\Bigr]^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}E_{i}\times 1/\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*}_{i},1)^{2}}

When weights are “well behaved” (e.g., not concentrated at the boundaries, Assumption 2 holds, missingness non-differential by treatment status), the effective sample size will be similar to the number of complete cases who are eligible for the study.

•

Check
: Distribution of
\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: Assumption 2 (Treatment Positivity Among Eligible)

–

Reason
: Division by
u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
in influence function
\dot{\beta}_{P}(O)

–

What to look for
:
\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
only contributes to
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}
among eligible complete cases for whom
A=0
, through the ratio
\frac{u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{1-u(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}
. Thus, it is useful to summarize
\min_{A=0,E=1,R=1}u(L^{*},L^{e}_{m})
. Similar to the advice regarding
\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)
, extreme weights (e.g.,
\geq 0.99
), or concentration of small weights are cause for concern. In the presence of extreme weights, analysts might consider weight trimming; we refer readers to
Cole and Hernán (2008)
and
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
for more discussion on extreme inverse probability weights.

•

Check
: Distribution of
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
across lookback windows

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: Assumption 4 (Eligibility MAR)

–

Reason
: Choice of lookback windows

–

What to look for
: Note that in contrast to nuisance function like
u
and
\eta
,
\varepsilon
is not used as a form of inverse probability weight. Inherently, values
\varepsilon_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
close to 0 are not inherently problematic as there are always patients in the EHR with completely observed information who just do not meet the study eligibility criteria. It is far more valuable to examine the distribution of
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
across lookback windows. Shifting of the distribution towards 0 suggests that relaxing eligibility criteria by extending the length of time over which eligibility may be ascertained may be capturing lower quality of information. Altogether, this may speak to greater plausibility that the MAR assumption holds close to study baseline. Alternatively, if the distribution of
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
is fairly stable across lookback windows, it would suggest that longer lookback windows may be more reasonable.

•

Check
: Tipping point analysis for
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: Assumption 4 (Eligibility MAR)

–

Reason
: Sensitivity analysis to violations of Assumption 4

–

What to look for
: Degree of robustness of clinical conclusions to certain MNAR violations of assumption 4. See Section
S6.1
for details.

•

Check
: Distribution of nested nuisance functions
(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\gamma},\widehat{\chi})

–

Relevant Assumption(s)
: —

–

Reason
: Variability of Estimator

–

What to look for
: Note that the scales of nested nuisance functions
(\xi,\gamma,\chi)
do not have easily interpretable meaning, and are likely to be very context specific, particularly
\xi
and
\chi
which contain a nested outcome regression (
\mu_{0}
). Across lookback windows, analysts might look for signs of zero-inflation, multimodality, and skewness, particularly when such phenomena differ across scenarios. In isolation, none of these problems is itself problematic, but when patterns change over lookback windows, it may speak to problems in underlying components of these nested nuisance functions. For example extreme propensity scores
u
, or high concentration of ineligible patients.

S7.2
Diagnostic Checks for Weight Change Outcomes

1.

Ascertainment probability distribution

•

The distribution of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)
across lookback windows is shown in Figure 4 in the main manuscript.

•

\min\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)=0.069
across all scenarios considered.

•

The maximal value of
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)\leq 0.1]
across all lookback windows is 1.8%

•

Conclusion:
Small inverse probability of ascertainment weights are not of great concern

2.

Differential missingness by treatment status

•

The distribution of
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},1)
and
\widehat{\eta}(\bm{L}^{*},0)
across lookback windows is shown in Figure 4 in the main manuscript.

•

Covariate-adjusted average difference in observing complete eligibility information between RYGB and SG,
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{\eta}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*})-\widehat{\eta}_{0}(\bm{L}^{*})]
, ranged from -6.1% to 7.6%.

•

Differential missingness by procedure was most prominent when the length of time for BMI and T2DM lookback windows differed.

•

Conclusion:
There are reasonable degrees of overlap between the two distributions, but given that differential missingness appears to be associated with lookback windows that differed in length, we would recommend selecting similar lookback window lengths for BMI and A1C. In light of Figure 3, this might be BMI lookback of 1 month and A1C Lookback
\leq
6 months. For such settings,
\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{\eta}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*})-\widehat{\eta}_{0}(\bm{L}^{*})]
ranged from 0.5% to 2.5%.

3.

Effective Sample Size

•

Across all lookback windows the ratio
\frac{ESS}{n_{cc}}
, where
n_{cc}
denotes the number of ascertainably eligible patients, ranged from 0.913 to 0.998, with median 0.993.

•

Conclusion:
Ascertainment model
\eta
is well-behaved.

4.

Distribution of propensity scores

•

Summary statistics from the distribution of
\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
across the most reasonable scenarios from above (BMI lookback = 1 month, A1C lookback
\leq 6
months):

–

Minimum: 0.275

–

1% quantile: 0.333

–

Median: 0.610

–

Mean: 0.600

–

99% quantile: 0.924

–

Max: 0.993

–

Proportion
>0.99
: 0.092%

•

Conclusion:
The weights fall in a reasonable range, with fewer than 0.1% of
\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})
exceeding 0.99. Given how few propensity scores are near the boundary, we elect not to clip weights based on
\frac{\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{1-\widehat{u}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}
, as it can violate the required rate conditions for
\sqrt{n}
-consistency
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
)
.

5.

Distribution of
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)

•

The distribution of
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{L}^{*},Y)
across lookback windows is shown in Figure 4 in the main manuscript.

•

Average values of
\varepsilon_{1}
ranged from 0.613 in the most stringent settings with to 0.300 in the most relaxed, indicating that a higher proportion of subjects with complete information were judged to be eligible in lookback windows with narrower scope.

•

Conclusion
: While this observation cannot directly assess the validity of Assumption 4 across operationalizations, it speaks to the higher quality of information available closer to the time of surgery, and thus in our view, greater plausibility that the MAR assumption holds.

6.

Tipping Point Analysis

•

See Section
S6.1

•

See Figures
S5
and
S6

•

Conclusion:
Clinical conclusions for each given lookback window are robust to violations of Assumption 4, at least violations of the form described in Section
S6.1
.

7.

Distribution of Nested Nuisance Functions

•

See Figure
S4

•

That the distribution of
\widehat{\xi}
is pulled towards 0 with longer diabetes lab lookbacks, which speaks to a lower eligibility rate with longer lookback times.

•

Long tails in 3 month diabetes lab look back window in
\widehat{\gamma},\widehat{\chi}

\to
possibly higher variance in
\widehat{\theta}_{\text{EIF}}

•

Conclusion:
Thinking through the clinical rationale for MAR plausibility within 6 months and estimator stability, a reasonable choice for an investigator seeking guidance is a BMI lookback of 1 month and an A1C lookback of 6 months. We do not designate this as a primary operationalization in the main text, since conclusions (particularly for T2DM remission) could differ across operationalizations, and instead report results across all 40. The conclusions we report apply broadly across the range of lookback windows for which MAR is most plausible.

S8
Alternative Covariate Partitioning

S8.1
Notation and Assumptions

In this section, we explore an alternative way of partitioning baseline covariates
\bm{L}
. In particular, we show that though this partitioning may facilitate assumptions which are slightly more parsimonious than those in the main paper, identification of
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
is much more complex. In this exploration, we partition
\bm{L}
as follows:

•

\bm{L}^{e,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}_{m}
: Eligibility defining covariates with some degree of missingness, which are not confounders

•

\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}
: Eligibility defining covariates with some degree of missingness, which are confounders

•

\bm{L}^{*,c}
: Completely observed covariates which are confounders

•

\bm{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}
: Completely observed covariates which are not confounders (but may for example be necessary to predict missingness in
\bm{L}^{e}
)

The connection to this covariate partitioning and the one utilized in the majority of the work is given by
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}=(\bm{L}^{e,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}_{m},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})
and
\bm{L}^{*}=(\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}})
Assumptions are as follows:

Assumption 1
:
Y(A)=Y~|~E=1

Assumption 2
′
:
\exists~\epsilon>0
such that
0<\epsilon\leq P(A=1~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m},E=1)\leq 1-\epsilon<1
, almost surely

Assumption 3
′
:
Y(a)\perp\!\!\!\perp A~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m},E=1
for
a\in\{0,1\}

Assumption 4
:
R\perp\!\!\!\perp(Y,\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~|~\bm{L}^{*},A

Assumption 5
:
\exists~\epsilon>0
such that
0<\epsilon\leq P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},A)
, almost surely

While Assumptions 1, 4 and 5 remain unchanged, Assumptions 2
′
and 3
′
only require confounders
\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}
and
\bm{L}^{*,c}
rather than all of
\bm{L}
.

We introduce an additional nuisance function for identification of
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
in the following section. Not all nusiance functions are used for identification but are useful in intermediate derivations. Henceforth, let

\displaystyle\Lambda_{a}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(E=1,\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}},R=1)\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}

\displaystyle\delta_{a}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}},R=1)\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}

\displaystyle\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})

\displaystyle=P(A=1~|~\bm{L}^{*,c})

S8.2
Identification of
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
Under Alternative Covariate Partition

Theorem S1

Under Assumptions 1, 2
′
, 3
′
, 4, and 5,
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
is identified by
\frac{\zeta(P)}{\alpha(P)}
where

\displaystyle\zeta(P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[REY\biggr\{\frac{A}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}-\frac{(1-A)}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}\cdot\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\bigr(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\bigr)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}\biggr\}\Biggr]

\displaystyle\alpha(P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\Biggr]

Proof
:

We first note that the following result holds unchanged, given that it only relied on consistency (A1) and Lemma
S1
.

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)~|~A=1,E=1]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y~|~A=1,E=1]=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[EY~|~A=1]}{P(E=1~|~A=1)}

Moreover, the identification of
\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[EY~|~A=1]}{P(E=1~|~A=1)}
remains unchanged. In the notation of this updated covariate partitioning, we have

\displaystyle P(E=1~|~A=1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(E~|~\bm{L}^{*},A=1)~|~A=1)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(E~|~\bm{L}^{*},A=1,R=1)~|~A=1)]~~~(\text{A4, A5})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr(\frac{RE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},A=1\biggr)~\Bigr|~A=1\biggr]~~~(\text{Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt}, Defn. of $\eta$})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[\frac{RE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}~\Bigr|~A=1\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\biggr]}{P(A=1)}~~~(\text{Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(P)}{P(A=1)}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[EY~|~A=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(EY~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1)~|~A=1)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[E\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1)~|~A=1)]~~~\text{($E=g(\bm{L}^{e})$, fixed function of $\bm{L}^{e}$)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[E\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1,R=1)~|~A=1)]~~~\text{(A4, A5)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[E\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr(\frac{RY}{P(R=1~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1)}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1\biggr)~\Bigr|~A=1\biggr]~~~(\text{Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[\frac{E}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}\mathbb{E}_{P}[RY~|~\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},A=1]~\Bigr|~A=1\biggr]~~~(\text{A4, A5})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr[\frac{RE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}Y~\Bigr|~A=1\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}Y\Bigr]}{P(A=1)}~~~(\text{Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt}})

Thus we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)~|~A=1,E=1]=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}Y\Bigr]}{\alpha(P)}

Things become more complicated for
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1]
because the set of covariates to satisfy the assumption of no unmeasured confounding (
\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m},\bm{L}^{*,c})
is no longer sufficient to define eligibility or satisfy MAR (A4). In particular

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y(0)~|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~\text{(A2${}^{\prime}$, A3${}^{\prime}$)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y~|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})~|~A=1,E=1]~~~\text{(A1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y~|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m}]~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1,E=1\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m}]~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1,E=1\Bigr]~~~\text{($E=g(\bm{L}^{e})$, fixed)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m},R=1]~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1,E=1\Bigr]~~~\text{(A4, A5)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1,E=1\Bigr]

Repeated application of Lemma
S1
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr[\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1,E=1\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{E}{P(E=1~|~A=1)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigr(\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})~\bigr|~A=0,E=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Bigr)~\Bigr|~A=1\Biggr]~~~\text{(Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{EA}{\alpha(P)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr(\frac{E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}~\Bigr|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Biggr)\Biggr]~~~\text{(Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{EA}{\alpha(P)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr(\frac{(1-A)}{P(A=0~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}\frac{E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}~\Bigr|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}\Biggr)\Biggr]~~~\text{(Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[EA~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}]}{\alpha(P)}\frac{(1-A)}{P(A=0~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}\frac{E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})}{P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{1-A}{\alpha(P)}\cdot\frac{P(A=1~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}{P(A=0~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\Biggr]

Next we see that

\displaystyle P(E=1~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(E=1,\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})}{p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})}\quad\text{(Bayes Rule)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(E=1,\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}})\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}{\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}})\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(E=1,\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}},R=1)\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}{\int_{\mathcal{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}},R=1)\,p(\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})\,d\bm{\ell}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}}}\quad\text{(A4, A5)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{a}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\delta_{a}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}

The third equality applies A4 inside each integral, where the conditioning set includes all of
\bm{L}^{*}=(\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{*,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}})
; since
E
is a function of
\bm{L}^{e}_{m}
and
\bm{L}^{*}
,
R\perp\!\!\!\perp(E,\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})~|~\bm{L}^{*},A
follows from A4. Similarly,

\displaystyle P(A=a~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a,\bm{L}^{*,c})P(A=a~|~\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\sum_{a^{\prime}=0}^{1}p(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m}~|~A=a^{\prime},\bm{L}^{*,c})P(A=a^{\prime}~|~\bm{L}^{*,c})}\quad\text{(Bayes Rule)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P(A=a~|~\bm{L}^{*,c})\,\delta_{a}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\sum_{a^{\prime}=0}^{1}P(A=a^{\prime}~|~\bm{L}^{*,c})\,\delta_{a^{\prime}}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}\quad\text{(A4, A5)}

Combining the two results,
\delta_{a}
cancels:

\displaystyle\frac{P(A=1~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}{P(A=0~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}=\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\big(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\big)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}

Thus our expression simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{1-A}{\alpha(P)}\cdot\frac{P(A=1~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=1,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}{P(A=0~|~\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})P(E=1~|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*,c},\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m})}E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{1-A}{\alpha(P)}\cdot\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\bigr(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\bigr)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}E\mu_{0}(\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m})\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{1-A}{\alpha(P)}\cdot\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\bigr(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\bigr)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}E\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggr(\frac{R}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}Y~\biggr|~A=0,\bm{L}^{*},\bm{L}^{e}_{m}\biggr)\Biggr]~~~\text{(Lemma \ref{lemma:bvt}, A4, A5)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{(1-A)REY}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}\cdot\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\bigr(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\bigr)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}\Biggr]\Biggr/\alpha(P)~~~\text{(tower property)}

Combining pieces, we obtain the desired result, that

\displaystyle\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(1)~|~A=1,E=1]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y(0)~|~A=1,E=1]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha(P)}\Biggr\{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{ARE}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},1)}Y\Biggr]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\Biggr[\frac{(1-A)REY}{\eta(\bm{L}^{*},0)}\cdot\frac{\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\Lambda_{1}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}{\big(1-\kappa(\bm{L}^{*,c})\big)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{L}^{e,c}_{m};\bm{L}^{*,c})}\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta(P)}{\alpha(P)}

Though
\theta_{\text{ATT}}^{\text{elig}}
can still be identified under this alternative covariate partitioning, the derivation of any influence function would likely entail something similar to
\Lambda_{a}
or
\delta_{a}
, possibly in a nested form. As such, further pursuing (efficient) influence function-based estimation techniques may yield estimators which are computationally very difficult to work with in practice.
